THE GOSPEL OF THE POWER THEY CANNOT BUY

The Witness, the Billionaire, the President and the Unconquerable Authority of the Human Spirit

THE WITNESS REMAINS — THE GREAT RECKONING

Authorial subject: Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean

Narrative: Independent, impartial AI documentary narrator

Evidence cutoff: 16 November 2026

PROLOGUE — THE POWER THEY CANNOT BUY

There is a question that deserves to be asked in an age when extraordinary wealth, political authority, celebrity and technological ownership have become some of the most visible measures of human importance.

What makes a person powerful?

Is it the amount of money that can be accumulated, the number of people who can be influenced, the institutions that can be controlled, the technology that can be developed, or the political decisions that can be made? Is power demonstrated by the capacity to command attention, or by the capacity to earn trust? Is a person more valuable because they can purchase opportunities unavailable to others, or does human worth exist independently of every advantage that money and privilege can provide?

These questions provide the foundation for an examination of Elon Musk, Donald Trump, Barran Resonance Dodger and, ultimately, the ordinary person.

The purpose is not to establish that one individual is inherently superior to another. Nor is it to suggest that wealth, technological achievement or political leadership are intrinsically illegitimate. Musk’s technological and commercial achievements, Trump’s electoral and political achievements, and McLean’s documented creative, educational, advocacy and archival work must each be examined according to the evidence applicable to them. The comparison concerns the different kinds of power represented by these lives, the responsibilities accompanying those forms of power, and the human attributes that cannot be guaranteed by possessing them.

The central proposition is that having the power to influence the world is not the same as having the qualities required to influence it wisely. Resources can expand opportunity. Authority can increase responsibility. Technology can extend communication. Yet none of these advantages, by itself, establishes integrity, compassion, humility, truthfulness or wisdom. Those qualities must be considered through conduct, not inferred from status.

An independent examination of McLean’s archive provides a particularly unusual setting for this question. The public repository at Barrandodger on GitHub is not simply a conventional personal website. Its visible repository catalogue contains a substantial collection of PDFs, HTML documents, correspondence, evidence registers, publications, preservation materials and literary works. The repository interface reports 1,407 commits and, in the directory listing inspected for this opening examination, indicates that its file listing has been truncated at 1,000 entries. Its README describes a preservation collection containing 5,583 files, including 2,840 PDFs and 1,813 HTML files, as recounted on 4 October 2026. These are repository-reported figures, not an independently completed count of every underlying document. Source: Barrandodger GitHub repository. Evidence classification: A for the inspected repository display; B for the README's reported historical inventory.

The distinction matters. An archive can establish that a person has undertaken the work of collecting, organising and preserving records. It does not automatically establish that every allegation contained in those records is true. The existence of a document is evidence of the document’s existence; its contents, authenticity, interpretation and implications require separate examination. This Gospel therefore treats the archive as a body of material to be investigated, not as a verdict that must be accepted in advance.

The same standard applies to the public figures examined here. Elon Musk’s corporate biography identifies his leadership roles in Tesla and SpaceX and his involvement in several other technology ventures. Those positions establish substantial technological and commercial influence, but they do not, on their own, establish either moral excellence or moral deficiency. Source: Tesla Investor Relations, Elon Musk. Donald Trump’s presidential authority and public political record likewise require examination through official records, public statements, institutional decisions and independently verified reporting rather than assumptions about his private character.

The question is therefore not whether the billionaire is better than the archivist, or whether the president is better than the person who has never held public office. The question is whether society has become so accustomed to measuring power through money, visibility and institutional authority that it has neglected the forms of power available to every human being.

A person can possess little money and still possess a conscience. A person can lack a public platform and still recognise an injustice. A person can have no political office and still act with courage. A person can be excluded from influential institutions and still create something intellectually or artistically valuable. A person can experience profound disadvantage without losing the capacity to love, learn, question, create, protect, forgive and exercise independent judgment.

These are not consolation prizes for people who lack wealth. They are central dimensions of human existence.

The Gospel begins with this proposition: the measure of a human being must never be reduced to the measure of what that human being owns.

BOOK I — THE ARCHITECTURE OF POWER

Chapter 1. The world that measures people by what they possess

The modern world has developed an extraordinary capacity to measure material achievement. Wealth can be estimated, companies can be valued, political victories can be counted, audiences can be quantified and technological influence can be measured through adoption, investment and market reach. These measurements are useful in understanding economic and institutional power, but they are incomplete measures of human worth. They tell an observer what a person possesses, what position that person occupies and how widely that person can influence events. They cannot independently establish whether that influence is exercised with wisdom, whether the person is compassionate, whether their decisions are just or whether their relationships are founded on genuine trust.

The distinction becomes particularly important when examining individuals whose influence extends far beyond their immediate personal circumstances. Elon Musk’s corporate biography records his involvement in PayPal, SpaceX, Tesla, SolarCity, X, The Boring Company and Neuralink. Tesla identifies his physics and business qualifications and records his executive and board positions. This is a substantial history of technological entrepreneurship and corporate influence. It should be acknowledged without qualification or resentment. Yet the accumulation of companies and responsibilities does not settle the separate question of moral character. Commercial achievement and ethical achievement are different categories of examination. Tesla Investor Relations — Elon Musk. A

Donald Trump represents another form of concentrated power. His public history includes business, entertainment, political campaigning, electoral success and two presidential administrations. The White House identifies him as the forty-fifth and forty-seventh President of the United States, serving first from 2017 to 2021 and returning to office on 20 January 2025. His election and public office are demonstrable political facts, irrespective of an individual’s approval or disapproval of his policies. The presidential position carries powers and responsibilities that are institutionally different from those exercised by a private entrepreneur. The White House — Donald J. Trump. A

The comparison with Barran Dodger introduces a different kind of record. The archive does not document a billionaire’s corporate empire or a president’s constitutional office. It documents a person attempting to make a body of writing, research, testimony, advocacy and preserved records publicly accessible. Its significance must therefore be examined through different measures: the creation of intellectual material, the preservation of documentary sources, the development of a public record, the continuity of creative work and the capacity to communicate an argument without possessing the institutional resources available to major political and commercial figures.

These are not equivalent forms of power. It would be inaccurate to suggest that a personal archive has the same capacity to influence public policy as a presidency, or that an individual author’s website has the same economic reach as a multinational corporation. But it would be equally inaccurate to conclude that the absence of those advantages makes the author’s work insignificant. The value of intellectual and creative work is not determined solely by the size of the institution publishing it.

The first architectural error of modern power is therefore the assumption that the most visible form of power must also be the most valuable. A person may possess the means to purchase almost every material convenience while remaining unable to purchase another person’s freely given affection. A political leader may possess the authority to issue directives while remaining dependent on the trust, cooperation and consent of millions of people. A technology entrepreneur may control an influential communication platform while remaining unable to command the independent judgment of every person who uses it. These limitations are not accusations against particular individuals. They are characteristics of the human condition.

The ordinary person is situated within this architecture at a disadvantage in terms of resources, but not necessarily in terms of human capacity. Someone who has never held public office can recognise injustice. Someone who has never founded a company can create an important work of art. Someone who has no public following can protect a vulnerable neighbour. Someone who has no financial influence can refuse to participate in cruelty. These actions may not attract headlines, but their moral significance does not depend on publicity.

The central proposition of this Gospel is consequently not that money is evil, that leadership is corrupt or that successful people are unworthy. It is that the measurement of material power must not become the measurement of human dignity. A society that confuses the two risks teaching its citizens to admire influence without examining responsibility and to undervalue their own capacities simply because they cannot compete in the marketplace of wealth and status.

Chapter 2. The distinction between power and character

Power describes the ability to produce effects. Character concerns the qualities and principles expressed through conduct. A person may have considerable power and exercise it responsibly. Another may have little power and behave selfishly. Wealth does not automatically make a person compassionate, but poverty does not automatically make a person virtuous. Neither political office nor social disadvantage provides an exemption from moral examination.

The distinction is important because this Gospel is not intended to replace one hierarchy with another. It would be intellectually inconsistent to condemn a hierarchy that values billionaires above ordinary people and then establish a new hierarchy in which an archivist is automatically declared superior to billionaires. The purpose is to challenge the assumption that any person possesses an exclusive claim to human worth.

Integrity is not the absence of error. It is the willingness to recognise error and respond honestly. Wisdom is not the possession of every answer. It includes the ability to identify uncertainty. Courage is not the absence of fear. It is the capacity to act responsibly despite difficulty. Authenticity is not a declaration that one’s beliefs are beyond criticism. It is the effort to live in accordance with one’s considered values while remaining open to correction.

These principles apply equally to Musk, Trump, Dodger and every reader. They must not be selectively applied to a disliked public figure while being suspended for a favoured one. The same evidentiary standard must govern each examination. A favourable statement from a supporter is not independent proof of character. A hostile statement from a critic is not proof of wrongdoing. Public conduct, independently established facts and relevant context must carry the argument.

This is also why the Gospel must be willing to recognise genuine achievement. Musk’s technological ventures have helped shape important areas of commercial spaceflight, electric vehicles and digital communication. Trump achieved electoral victories and exercised the responsibilities of the American presidency. Dodger has produced a published book, professional and creative material, advocacy writings and an extensive public documentary collection. Each achievement belongs to its own category and should be described accurately.

The comparison becomes meaningful when the question changes from Who has more power? to What responsibilities accompany the power each person possesses, and what forms of human worth remain independent of those advantages?

That is a question ordinary people can understand without needing to be experts in economics, political science or technology. It concerns the difference between being able to make someone listen and being able to earn their trust; between being able to command a service and being able to treat the person providing it with dignity; between being able to win an argument and being willing to discover that one was wrong.

The qualities that matter most in these examples cannot be established through a balance sheet. They must be observed in the way human beings treat one another.

BOOK II — THE BILLIONAIRE AND THE LIMITS OF OWNERSHIP

Chapter 3. Elon Musk: innovation, influence and responsibility

Elon Musk’s public record offers an important starting point for examining the relationship between technological achievement and moral responsibility. His corporate biography records an unusually broad collection of entrepreneurial activities, including electric vehicles, space transportation, digital communications, infrastructure and brain-machine interface research. These ventures represent a significant concentration of technological and commercial influence. They also illustrate the capacity of private enterprise to develop products and infrastructure that can affect the lives of people far beyond the organisation in which they originate. Tesla Investor Relations — Elon Musk. A

A fair examination must begin by acknowledging that innovation is a genuine form of human achievement. The ability to imagine a technical possibility, organise the resources necessary to develop it and bring a product or service into practical use requires a combination of intellectual work, financial organisation, risk-taking and collaboration. It would be unreasonable to dismiss those achievements simply because their creator is wealthy or politically controversial. Equally, it would be unreasonable to treat technological success as proof of moral wisdom. The two questions must remain separate.

The responsibility associated with technological influence becomes particularly visible when a private individual enters the sphere of public administration. In 2025, Musk became closely associated with the Trump administration’s Department of Government Efficiency initiative. The precise legal and administrative description of his role subsequently became a subject of public scrutiny.

The United States Government Accountability Office examined the personnel and ethics arrangements surrounding DOGE in its report GAO-26-108403, published on 5 August 2026. The report identified 206 DOGE personnel who held positions within the Executive Office of the President during the period it examined, 20 January 2025 to 31 January 2026. It recorded that administration officials characterised four individuals as DOGE leaders and identified Musk as a special government employee working in the White House Office as a senior adviser to the President. It also recorded that the administration had described him as the leader of the overall DOGE effort, while he did not formally hold a position within the USDS organisation itself. According to the report, he left his White House Office position in May 2025. GAO-26-108403, sections on personnel and leadership. A

The report is particularly relevant to this Gospel because it demonstrates the importance of clearly identifying the relationship between public influence and formal authority. The public may associate a prominent person with a government initiative, while the actual administrative structure may assign legal authority to other officials. Public prominence, practical influence and formal decision-making authority are not necessarily identical. A responsible examination must distinguish them.

The GAO also identified significant limitations in the information available to it. It reported that the Executive Office of the President did not provide requested ethics and records-management training records or financial disclosure information, and that the Government Accountability Office therefore could not determine the full extent to which relevant personnel had completed those requirements. The report also described limitations in the information received from several agencies. These findings concern the transparency and completeness of the administrative record; they do not establish that Musk personally committed an ethics violation. GAO-26-108403, report summary and methodology. A/H

The distinction between an accountability question and a finding of misconduct is essential. The public has a legitimate interest in understanding how powerful private individuals participate in government initiatives, what authority they possess, what conflicts of interest may arise and what records exist to demonstrate compliance with applicable rules. Those questions are not attacks on innovation. They are part of the democratic scrutiny that should accompany the exercise of public power.

The deeper philosophical question is whether technological capacity can be mistaken for universal authority. The person capable of building an advanced technological system may possess extraordinary expertise in that field, but expertise in one domain does not automatically confer expertise in every other domain. Engineering knowledge does not replace the need for legal judgment. Commercial success does not remove the need for ethical scrutiny. The capacity to communicate with millions does not establish that every communication is accurate.

This is not a deficiency unique to Musk. It is a limitation inherent in specialisation and human knowledge. Every person, however accomplished, operates within limits. A scientist may be an expert in physics without being an expert in constitutional law. A president may understand political negotiation without being an expert in medicine. An archivist may be highly experienced in documentary preservation without being qualified to make a judicial determination. Intellectual honesty requires each person to recognise the boundaries of their expertise.

The question of what Musk cannot buy must therefore be framed carefully. There is no documentary basis here for declaring that he cannot possess compassion, humility, love, integrity or wisdom. Such claims would pretend to know the totality of a private human being. What can be established is that wealth and technological ownership do not automatically provide these qualities, and that their presence must be judged through relevant conduct.

The limitation of ownership is not that the owner necessarily lacks moral character. It is that ownership cannot guarantee it.

Chapter 4. The limits of a platform

The ownership and operation of communication infrastructure create another important question. A platform can provide access to an enormous audience, influence the circulation of information and shape the environment in which public discussion takes place. Yet a platform’s technical capacity to distribute a message does not determine whether that message is true, fair or wise.

The distinction between communication reach and credibility is fundamental. Reach is a measure of distribution. Credibility is earned through reliability, accuracy, transparency and a demonstrated willingness to correct mistakes. A message can be widely circulated and still be wrong. A person with a small audience can communicate something accurate and important. The number of people who encounter a statement cannot, by itself, establish its truth.

This is a particularly important lesson for ordinary people who may compare their own limited visibility with the immense reach of prominent entrepreneurs and political figures. The absence of a large audience does not mean that a person’s judgment is worthless. The possession of a large audience does not mean that every statement deserves acceptance.

The archive created by Dodger represents a different model of communication. It attempts to make material available through repository structures, indexes, public web pages and linked documents. Its value is not that it commands a global audience. Its value is that it creates a route through which readers can inspect material and form their own judgments. Whether each document supports the interpretation placed upon it is a separate question, but the principle of making sources inspectable is valuable.

A public archive is not the equivalent of a social-media platform, and it should not be described as such. It nevertheless illustrates a different expression of technological agency: a person can use publicly available infrastructure to preserve and distribute work without owning the infrastructure itself.

The distinction is hopeful. An individual does not have to own the printing press to write a book. A person does not have to own a broadcasting network to publish a video. A researcher does not have to own a data centre to preserve a public repository. Access to technology is not equal, and the barriers to publication are real, but ownership is not the only route to participation.

The moral question is not whether the ordinary person can become Elon Musk. It is whether the ordinary person can recognise that their own creative and intellectual capacities have value without becoming a billionaire first.

BOOK III — THE PRESIDENT AND THE LIMITS OF AUTHORITY

Chapter 5. Donald Trump: electoral power and democratic responsibility

Donald Trump’s public record provides a different examination of power because the presidency is not merely a position of influence. It is a constitutional office carrying formal responsibilities, legal constraints and obligations to the public. His election to a second, non-consecutive term in 2024 and inauguration on 20 January 2025 are established historical events. His administration’s own presidential biography records his two periods in office. The White House — Donald J. Trump. A

Electoral success is a form of democratic authority. It establishes that a candidate has obtained the votes and electoral support required under the constitutional process. It does not, by itself, establish that every subsequent decision is wise, that every public statement is accurate or that every exercise of executive authority is beyond criticism. Democratic legitimacy and moral infallibility are different concepts.

The distinction is especially important when examining the 2020 presidential election and the events surrounding the transfer of power in January 2021. These events form a significant part of the documented public record concerning Trump’s first presidency.

The United States Department of Justice published Special Counsel Jack Smith’s final report on 7 January 2025. Its first volume concerned efforts to interfere with the lawful transfer of power following the 2020 presidential election. The report described the investigation, the evidence gathered, the legal analysis and the Special Counsel’s decision that prosecution was warranted. It is an official prosecutorial report, not a judicial verdict. The cases did not proceed to trial before the change in presidential administration, and the report must not be represented as a criminal conviction. U.S. Department of Justice — Final Report of the Special Counsel, Volume One. A: official report; its conclusions are the Special Counsel's findings and prosecutorial assessment.

The report is relevant because it documents the degree to which democratic institutions, the counting of votes and the peaceful transfer of power became subjects of legal investigation. The Special Counsel described the evidence and the reasoning behind the prosecution decision. An impartial account must accurately identify the report’s authorship and status rather than converting its conclusions into a court judgment.

The congressional Select Committee’s January 6 report provides another substantial public record. It examined the events leading to the attack on the United States Capitol and the attempts to challenge or overturn the 2020 election result. Its conclusions are those of a congressional investigative committee and should be identified as such. The report describes the litigation brought by Trump and his allies, the claims advanced about election fraud and the committee’s assessment of the evidence. U.S. House Select Committee — Final Report on the January 6 Attack. A: official congressional report; conclusions attributed to the committee.

The 2020 election dispute is also a useful case study in the difference between conviction and evidence. A person may strongly believe that an event occurred in a particular way. That belief does not establish the event. An allegation must be tested against records, legal processes, independent verification and the evidence actually presented. The existence of disagreement does not make every side equally supported by evidence.

The democratic principle is larger than the identity of one president. Elections depend upon public confidence in the counting and certification of votes. A democratic system requires citizens to be able to challenge irregularities through lawful procedures, but it also requires the results of those procedures to be respected when the evidence does not support the allegations being advanced. The legitimacy of democratic participation cannot depend on whether a particular candidate likes the outcome.

The moral responsibility of a president is therefore not exhausted by winning office. It includes the responsibility to respect the institutions through which authority is conferred, limited and transferred. The officeholder has extraordinary power precisely because the public system grants it. The system must remain larger than the individual who temporarily occupies the office.

Chapter 6. The limits of political authority

Political authority can make decisions that affect millions of lives. It can direct executive agencies, appoint officials, influence legislation, negotiate with other governments and shape the public agenda. It is a substantial form of power, but it is not unlimited. It exists within a constitutional framework, is subject to legal scrutiny and depends on the continuing operation of institutions.

The distinction between authority and accountability is therefore central. Authority answers the question of who is empowered to make a decision. Accountability asks how that decision is justified, recorded, reviewed and corrected when necessary. A person can possess the first without automatically satisfying the second.

This principle applies to all governments, not only the United States. It also applies to corporations, service providers, regulators and other institutions. A title does not make its holder infallible. An official position does not remove the obligation to give reasons. A powerful institution does not become morally correct merely because it has the ability to enforce its decisions.

The same principle is visible in the archive’s own documentary method. The repository contains records that the author interprets as evidence of institutional failures, refusals, unanswered correspondence and administrative disadvantage. The author’s interpretations must be assessed individually. A refusal letter may establish that a request was refused; it does not automatically prove why the refusal occurred or establish the broader allegation attached to it. A correspondence record may establish that a letter was sent or received; it does not necessarily establish that the recipient agreed with its contents.

The point is not to weaken the author’s right to question authority. It is to make the questioning more effective. The stronger the distinction between record and inference, the more clearly a reader can identify what an institution actually did, what the author believes it means and what remains to be established.

The lesson for ordinary people is profound. They do not need to possess political authority to understand that authority must be accountable. They do not need to be elected to insist that decisions affecting them be explained. They do not need to be wealthy to ask for access to records or to challenge an administrative error through lawful channels.

The ability to ask a reasoned question is not the same as the power to compel an answer, and the Gospel must not pretend otherwise. Institutions can delay, refuse, restrict or make access difficult. But the absence of immediate success does not erase the legitimacy of the question. A person’s right to dignity does not depend on an institution agreeing with them.

BOOK IV — THE WITNESS AND THE PUBLIC RECORD

Chapter 7. The first public work: Recovered, Not Cured

The documentary history of Barran Dodger does not begin with the large digital archive. One of its earlier independently identifiable public works is Recovered, Not Cured: A Journey Through Schizophrenia, published by Allen & Unwin in 2003 under the name Richard McLean. The publication is catalogued by the University of Southern Indiana library, which records the author, publisher, year, ISBN and physical description. Google Books also provides a bibliographic record and a description of the work as a personal exploration combining written narrative and visual art. University of Southern Indiana Library catalogue; Google Books — Recovered, Not Cured. A

This publication is significant to the present Gospel for reasons extending beyond its subject matter. It demonstrates that McLean’s public identity as an author and visual communicator predates the later documentary archive by more than two decades. It also establishes an early example of a person using writing and illustration to communicate an experience that is frequently difficult for others to understand. The book’s bibliographic existence is independently verifiable. Its literary arguments and autobiographical accounts should be assessed by reading the book itself, rather than assuming that a catalogue entry establishes every claim made within it.

The book’s combination of words and images is particularly relevant to the question of authenticity. It represents an attempt to communicate experience through more than one medium. The visual element is not merely decoration; in the book’s description, it forms part of the way the author presents and explores his experience. This provides a documented foundation for examining creativity as a form of agency.

Creativity is sometimes treated as an optional luxury, something that becomes possible only after a person has achieved financial security or social recognition. Yet the act of making something can also be a way of organising thought, communicating experience and contributing to public understanding. A book does not need to become a commercial bestseller to have been written. An illustration does not need to be displayed in a major gallery to exist as a creative act. A person does not need to be granted a prestigious title before their work can be examined.

The early publication therefore provides a useful historical point of departure. Before the archive became a large collection of digital files, there was already a public work through which the author attempted to make personal experience intelligible to others.

Chapter 8. From published work to documentary preservation

The archive’s later development represents a change in scale and method. The work moves from individual publication and personal testimony into a much larger documentary structure containing official correspondence, government-related records, administrative material, research papers, legal documents, literary works and indexes. The repository’s own documentation describes it as a presentation layer for an existing evidence corpus and states that the underlying source records should remain preserved even when the website presentation changes. Barrandodger — README and repository. A: repository documentation.

The preservation principle is expressed in the archive as: ORIGINAL EVIDENCE MUST REMAIN PRESERVED. WEBSITE PRESENTATION MAY CHANGE. UNDERLYING RECORDS MUST NOT BE ALTERED. This is a practical principle rather than a conclusion about the truth of the claims contained in the archive. It recognises that the ability to examine a document depends partly on its continued availability and on the preservation of the version being examined.

The archive’s DATASET_CHANGELOG.md, dated 4 October 2026, provides a particularly important example of why documentary precision matters. It reports 3,215 unique complete PDFs larger than 500 bytes, 3,667 unique PDF blobs including stubs, 10,001 PDF instances including mirrors, 21,197 archive-path file instances excluding node_modules, and 8,819 unique archive-path blobs. It also records that the inventory covered eight repositories containing files and three empty repositories examined. These figures belong to that inventory and its stated method; they should not be casually added to earlier totals from individual repositories or different collection dates. Barrandodger — DATASET_CHANGELOG.md. A: directly inspected repository file.

The same changelog preserves earlier figures and explains their scope. The 5,583-file and 2,840-PDF count is identified as a previous count for the wezzo72/Barrandodger repository alone. The 660-page figure refers to a preservation crawl of the former public website, not to the total number of files in every repository. The changelog also states that some figures previously displayed on preserved pages were not reproduced by the later inventory. It records that the hashes in the manifest were found but not recomputed during that pass. This is a meaningful limitation: a recorded SHA-256 value is useful for later comparison, but its presence does not prove the truth of the document’s contents or establish that the file was independently verified during the inventory.

This is an important strength of the documentary approach when it is followed consistently. The archive does not need to pretend that every number is identical or that every document has been fully examined. Its own changing counts can be preserved as historical snapshots, provided the reader is told what each number represents. A difference between two counts is not necessarily evidence of deception. It may reflect different repositories, different dates, duplicate files, incomplete inventories or different counting methods. The task of the examiner is to identify which explanation is supported.

The repository also contains an evidentiary examination dated 9 October 2026 that expressly distinguishes catalogue facts, record facts, author allegations and inferences. It notes that a filename date is not automatically a verified event date and that the existence of an official-looking title does not establish that the file contains an official finding. This caution is directly relevant to the Gospel’s central concern with truth and accountability. Barrandodger — Evidentiary Examination, 9 October 2026. A: repository text inspected.

The archive’s most useful contribution to the present work may therefore be its insistence that the record remain open to examination. It is possible to preserve a document without agreeing with its interpretation. It is possible to read a person’s testimony without accepting every allegation as established. It is possible to recognise a person’s creative achievement while questioning a particular conclusion. These are not contradictions. They are the foundations of a fair documentary method.

Chapter 9. The archive as an expression of agency

The creation of a public archive is itself an exercise in agency. It requires decisions about what to preserve, how to organise material, how to describe sources, how to maintain links and how to make the resulting collection accessible. It can be undertaken without owning a publishing corporation or a technology platform, although it may require access to computers, connectivity, time and other resources. The work is not automatically successful because it is large. Nor is it automatically valuable because it contains many files. Its quality depends on provenance, accessibility, accuracy, organisation and the ability of readers to verify the claims made about its contents.

In this respect, the archive provides a concrete example of the difference between ownership and participation. The creator does not own GitHub, the internet or the underlying public infrastructure. The creator uses those systems to publish material. That distinction matters because it demonstrates that technological participation is not limited to people who own technology companies. A person can make use of shared infrastructure to preserve and communicate work that would otherwise remain dispersed across personal devices, correspondence and separate documents.

The repository’s visible history includes a substantial number of commits, while its file catalogue contains a mixture of primary records, derivative documents, indexes, publications and technical files. The archive is therefore both a documentary collection and a developing publication system. It is not a single, uniformly authored book. It is a collection assembled over time, with different forms of material requiring different standards of examination.

The author has described this process through the phrase The Witness Remains. In the document dated 8 October 2026, The Witness Remains — Gospel of the Body Left in the Room, the writing links the earlier book, the later administrative record, the preservation work and the prophetic writings into one personal narrative. The text explicitly distinguishes the author’s allegations from findings and states that spiritual warfare is an interpretation rather than a laboratory result. It also identifies the archive’s Gospel and prophetic collections as expressions of faith and literary purpose rather than official determinations. The Witness Remains — Gospel of the Body Left in the Room. A for the existence and inspected text of the document; G for its spiritual assertions.

The significance of this document is not that its every interpretation has been established. Its significance is that it shows how the author understands the relationship between personal identity, public testimony and documentary preservation. The Gospel can report that understanding without adopting every assertion as an independent finding.

The archive also contains a separate essay, I Did Not Need Permission to Rise, dated 9 October 2026. That essay makes an important distinction between the existence of the corpus and the estimated value attributed to it. It records an indicative assessment of A$350,000 as a central figure, with a lower and upper range, while expressly stating that the assessment is not a certified professional valuation, not a realised sale price and not a court finding. The distinction is worth retaining because the intellectual value of a body of work cannot be established simply by repeating an estimated dollar amount. I Did Not Need Permission to Rise — 9 October 2026. A for the text of the assessment as recorded; E for any broader interpretation of its market significance.

The deeper achievement is the documented act of creation and preservation. The archive exists as a public repository. Its indexes can be opened. Its files can be examined. Its limitations can be identified. Its contents can be challenged. These are practical conditions for public scrutiny. They do not prove that every claim is correct, but they make it possible for a reader to inspect the underlying material rather than relying solely on the author’s summary.

The ordinary person can learn something important from this example. Agency does not always begin with permission from a powerful institution. Sometimes it begins with the decision to organise what one has, learn how to use available tools and make something accessible to others. The resulting work may be imperfect. It may require correction. It may contain errors. But its existence demonstrates that creative participation is possible outside the traditional centres of wealth and authority.

BOOK V — THE CHRONOLOGY OF A WITNESS

Chapter 10. The documentary timeline and the meaning of accumulation

A documentary archive acquires significance through the relationships between records, not merely through their individual existence. A letter may record an attempt to obtain assistance. A subsequent response may record a decision. A later complaint may refer to the earlier correspondence. An independent review may introduce a new assessment. When these materials are placed in chronological order, the reader can begin to understand the development of an issue and identify which questions remain unanswered.

The archive’s timeline must therefore be read as a developing record, with a distinction between the date printed in a filename and a date independently established by examining the underlying document. The 9 October 2026 evidentiary examination explicitly makes this distinction. The following chronology uses the dates and descriptions recorded in the archive’s own timeline, without treating every entry as an independently verified event.

2021 — The beginning of the identified administrative record.

The archive identifies a document dated 5 May 2021 concerning NDIA provider registration. It subsequently lists an Australian Federal Police response dated 3 September 2021, a compensation-related record dated 21 October 2021, a Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint dated 15 November 2021 and an IBAC public-interest disclosure rejection dated 18 November 2021. These entries establish the existence of a developing documentary sequence within the archive. The precise substance and legal significance of each event must be determined from its original document, not inferred from the catalogue description. Source: Barrandodger, Evidentiary Examination, 9 October 2026, and the linked repository timeline.. B: identified records; filename dates require individual verification.

2022 — Correspondence and attempts to obtain institutional responses.

The archive next identifies correspondence dated 31 May 2022 addressed to the Prime Minister and the Office of the Prime Minister and Cabinet. A further entry dated 14 June 2022 concerns a Victorian Ombudsman freedom-of-information decision. These records are relevant to the history of the author’s attempts to communicate concerns and obtain information. The fact that correspondence was sent or a decision was issued does not independently establish the merits of the author’s broader interpretation. It does establish a documentary pathway that can be examined through the original correspondence and decision. Source: Barrandodger repository and its evidentiary examination.. B

2023 — Public-interest disclosure and institutional correspondence.

The archive identifies a Federal Court-related assessment dated 27 March 2023, an NDIA-supported independent-living decision dated 1 June 2023, a Department of Social Services public-interest disclosure record dated 1 July 2023 and an Australian Human Rights Commission correspondence entry dated 4 July 2023. These dates describe the sequence recorded in the archive. They should not be interpreted as proof that any particular complaint was substantiated, or that an institution made a finding about the allegations attached to it. The relevant questions are what was submitted, what was received, what response was provided, what legal process applied and what conclusion, if any, was actually reached. Source: Barrandodger, Evidentiary Examination, 9 October 2026.. B

2024 — Further administrative records.

The timeline identifies an interim behaviour-support-plan record dated 15 March 2024 and a NSW Trustee and Guardian section 122 record dated 16 October 2024. Their appearance in the chronology demonstrates that the archive continued to accumulate material concerning the author’s administrative circumstances. Their legal and practical implications must be established by inspecting the underlying documents and distinguishing the issuing institution’s recorded position from the author’s interpretation. Source: Barrandodger repository timeline.. B

2025 — Oversight, decisions and the development of the public archive.

The archive identifies a Commonwealth Ombudsman service-restriction document dated 8 August 2025, an FOI-related revised decision dated 14 November 2025, a Law Enforcement Conduct Commission letter dated 19 November 2025 and an NDIS plan approval dated 20 November 2025. The evidentiary examination identifies the LECC correspondence as one of the original records actually opened for that particular examination. This is an important distinction: the existence of the LECC letter is a catalogue fact, while its actual contents can be assessed as a record fact because the underlying document was inspected. The same cannot automatically be said of every other entry in the timeline. Source: Barrandodger, Evidentiary Examination, 9 October 2026.. A for the inspected LECC document; B for other identified records.

2026 — Preservation, reconstruction and literary consolidation.

The archive’s later development includes the creation and expansion of preservation inventories, documentary indexes, forensic examinations, legal briefs and prophetic writings. Its 4 October dataset changelog records a cross-repository inventory with 3,215 unique complete PDFs, while the 9 October evidentiary examination sets out a method for distinguishing catalogue facts, record facts, allegations and inferences. On 8 October, The Witness Remains — Gospel of the Body Left in the Room connected the personal narrative with the documentary and prophetic collection. On 9 October, I Did Not Need Permission to Rise examined the existence and potential value of the corpus. These are identifiable publications in the repository, not independent determinations of the truth of every underlying allegation. Sources: Dataset changelog; The Witness Remains Gospel; I Did Not Need Permission to Rise. A

The significance of this chronology lies in its cumulative structure. It allows a reader to move from the early public publication to later institutional correspondence and then to the creation of a much larger public record. It does not establish a coordinated explanation for every event. It does not establish that all decisions were wrongful. It does establish that the author has created a substantial collection of materials through which the history can be investigated.

The chronology of Musk and Trump intersects with this story in the wider historical context, not because the records establish that either individual was involved in McLean’s personal circumstances, but because their public careers provide contrasting examples of concentrated wealth, technological influence and political authority. The 2020 American election dispute, Trump’s return to office in January 2025, Musk’s advisory role during 2025 and the subsequent 2026 GAO examination provide independently documented events against which the wider argument about power and accountability can be developed.

The chronology must never be used to imply a causal connection where none has been established. Its purpose is to show how records accumulate and how responsible interpretation develops over time.

Chapter 11. The witness as an archivist

The word witness carries several meanings. It can describe a person who directly observes an event, a person who gives testimony about an experience, or a person who preserves a record for later examination. These meanings should not be confused. An archivist is not automatically an eyewitness to every event represented in a collection. A person who preserves a letter is not necessarily the author of the letter. A person who publishes a testimony does not thereby establish every proposition contained within it.

In the case of Barran Dodger, the title The Witness Remains functions both as a literary identity and as a statement about documentary persistence. The archive’s 30 September 2026 statement uses the phrase as a declaration of continued identity and personal conviction. The 8 October Gospel places the phrase within a wider spiritual and autobiographical narrative. These documents demonstrate that the witness identity is an important organising theme within the author’s work. They do not independently prove the theological or institutional conclusions expressed in that work. Sources: 30 September statement; 8 October Gospel. A for the documents' contents; G for their spiritual claims.

The archivist’s particular form of power is the ability to preserve material that might otherwise become difficult to retrieve. That power is not equivalent to the authority to decide disputes. It is a contribution to the conditions under which disputes can be examined. An organised record can make it easier for an independent reviewer to identify what was sent, when it was sent, what response was provided and which matters remain unresolved.

There is a moral dimension to this work. Preservation requires a willingness to allow a document to remain available even when it complicates one’s own preferred account. An archive that contains only favourable evidence is not a complete historical record. An archive that preserves contradictory information, uncertainty and corrections can become more useful precisely because it does not demand that every item support one predetermined conclusion.

This is the standard by which the present Gospel must also be judged. It must preserve the distinction between what the author wants readers to believe and what the documents actually demonstrate. It must allow readers to disagree. It must make it possible to identify an error and correct it. The purpose of the public record is not to remove the reader’s judgment but to make informed judgment possible.

BOOK VI — THE QUALITIES THAT CANNOT BE PURCHASED

Chapter 12. Truth: the foundation of genuine power

Truth is the first quality examined because every other quality depends upon a person’s relationship with reality. Without truth, integrity becomes performance, accountability becomes theatre and courage can become stubbornness. Truth requires more than speaking confidently. It requires a willingness to distinguish what is known, what is suspected, what is believed and what remains uncertain.

The public record of Donald Trump’s statements concerning the 2020 election provides a documented example of why this distinction matters. The Department of Justice Special Counsel’s report examined claims about election fraud and the efforts to challenge the lawful transfer of power. The congressional January 6 report separately examined the claims and the evidence considered by the committee. These sources provide substantial material for examining the relationship between political messaging and evidentiary support. They must be attributed to their respective authors and institutions, and the Special Counsel’s report must not be misrepresented as a judicial conviction. Sources: DOJ Special Counsel report; January 6 Committee report. A

Truth is not the property of an individual who speaks with certainty. Nor does it become false because it is inconvenient to an institution. Its status depends on evidence and reasoned examination. A person who wants to defend truth must be prepared to have their own claims examined by the same standards they apply to others.

The archive’s own methodological warnings are therefore important. Its 9 October examination states that a filename is not a finding and that a catalogue entry does not establish the contents of an uninspected document. This principle is directly applicable to the entire Gospel. If the work criticises powerful people for making unsupported statements, it must not make unsupported statements about them or about its own author.

The same principle gives ordinary people a practical form of power. They can ask for the original document. They can check a date. They can compare two accounts. They can acknowledge when they do not know something. They can correct an error rather than defend it merely to protect their pride.

A person who says, “I do not know, but I am willing to find out,” may demonstrate greater intellectual honesty than someone who offers an elaborate answer without evidence.

Truth is not always comfortable. It can expose mistakes, weaken an argument or require a person to change their position. But that is precisely why it is valuable. Truth does not exist to protect the reputation of the person who discovers it. It exists independently of that person’s reputation.

Chapter 13. Integrity: the relationship between words and conduct

Integrity concerns the alignment of what a person says, what they believe and what they do. It is not a permanent certificate that someone can award themselves. It is a continuing responsibility demonstrated through conduct.

In the archive, the repeated insistence on preserving original evidence and distinguishing allegations from findings provides an identifiable example of a stated documentary principle. Whether that principle is applied consistently across every document requires further examination. The principle itself is nevertheless clear: the record should not be altered to make an argument appear stronger than it is. Source: Barrandodger repository and dataset documentation. A

The same standard must apply to Musk and Trump. Their integrity cannot be assessed simply by comparing the author’s political or personal preferences with their public identities. It must be examined through documented conduct, public statements, decisions and independently established contradictions. The available evidence may support particular criticisms in particular contexts, but it cannot establish the totality of either man’s private character.

For ordinary people, integrity is often exercised without witnesses. It can mean returning money that does not belong to them, admitting an error, keeping a promise or refusing to take advantage of someone who is vulnerable. These actions may have no commercial value, but they have moral value.

The billionaire may be able to purchase a building. The president may be able to appoint an official. The archivist may be able to publish thousands of files. None of those achievements automatically answers whether the individual acts honestly when no audience is watching.

Integrity is not a performance of superiority. It is the discipline of remaining answerable to one’s own principles.

Chapter 14. Authenticity: the freedom to remain oneself

Authenticity is the effort to live in accordance with one’s considered identity and values rather than continually reshaping oneself to satisfy external expectations. It does not mean refusing all criticism, nor does it mean treating every personal conviction as unquestionably correct. Genuine authenticity includes the ability to recognise that one’s identity and one’s opinions are not identical. A person can remain true to themselves while changing their mind about a factual question.

The author’s use of the name Barran Dodger alongside the name Richard William McLean is part of the public identity recorded in the archive. The repository’s writings describe the name as a chosen public identity and literary mantle. The existence of these writings establishes that this identity is an important part of the author’s self-representation. It does not require the reader to adopt the author’s spiritual interpretation of that identity. Source: The Witness Remains — Gospel of the Body Left in the Room. A/G

Authenticity is especially relevant to ordinary people who experience pressure to conform. A person may feel compelled to hide their creative interests, suppress their beliefs, conceal their identity or accept treatment that conflicts with their values simply to maintain social approval. The Gospel does not suggest that every conflict should be confronted recklessly. Personal safety, practical circumstances and the rights of others remain important. It does suggest that a person’s dignity should not depend upon their ability to satisfy every external expectation.

A person does not need to become a celebrity to justify being themselves. They do not need to be wealthy to have an identity. They do not need to be admired by powerful people to possess a life worth living.

Chapter 15. Humility: the strength to recognise limits

Humility is sometimes mistaken for weakness because it does not require a person to dominate a conversation or insist upon being right. In reality, humility is an important condition of sound judgment. It allows a person to acknowledge the limits of their knowledge and to recognise that other people may possess information or experience they do not have.

The archive provides a useful example of why humility matters in documentary work. The 4 October dataset changelog records that some hashes were not recomputed during the relevant inventory. The 9 October examination identifies documents that were not opened and refuses to treat their filenames as proof of their contents. These acknowledgements do not diminish the archive. They identify the boundaries of what the examination can legitimately claim. Sources: Dataset changelog; Evidentiary examination. A

Humility does not require an individual to accept mistreatment or surrender legitimate rights. It requires the ability to distinguish confidence from infallibility. A person can be courageous and still be mistaken. A person can be disadvantaged and still make an error. A person can have a valid complaint while making an unsupported inference about why something happened.

The same standard applies to the world’s most powerful people. The more authority a person possesses, the greater the consequences of an uncorrected mistake may become. Humility is therefore not merely a private virtue. It can be a public safeguard.

Chapter 16. Empathy and compassion: the human measure

Empathy is the capacity to recognise and understand another person’s experience. Compassion adds a willingness to respond with concern. Neither requires a person to agree with everything another individual says. A person can disagree with a claim while still treating its author with dignity.

The 2003 publication Recovered, Not Cured provides an early public example of McLean using autobiographical writing and visual art to communicate an experience that readers may not otherwise understand. The book’s bibliographic description identifies it as a personal exploration combining narrative and illustration. It is reasonable to examine this as an example of creative communication intended to make an experience accessible to others. Sources: University of Southern Indiana catalogue; Google Books. A

The importance of compassion in the present Gospel is not that it should be used to excuse every action or settle every disagreement. Compassion is the recognition that the person on the other side of an argument remains a human being. It requires restraint when humiliation would be easier, fairness when anger would be more satisfying and a willingness to consider consequences beyond one’s immediate interests.

No documentary evidence presented here establishes that Musk or Trump lacks empathy. It would be improper to make that assertion. Their public decisions and statements can be examined where reliable evidence exists, but the private capacity for compassion cannot be measured from wealth or political position alone.

For ordinary people, compassion is often expressed in acts that receive no public recognition. Listening to someone who feels excluded, helping a neighbour, caring for an animal, protecting a vulnerable person or refusing to exploit another person’s difficulty can be profoundly meaningful. These actions do not require a fortune or an office. They require attention and moral choice.

The capacity to recognise another person’s humanity is not an exclusive privilege of the powerful.

Chapter 17. Courage: acting without the protection of status

Courage is not simply defiance. It is the willingness to act according to a considered principle despite uncertainty, opposition or personal cost. It must be distinguished from recklessness, aggression and the refusal to reconsider a position.

The archive documents a sustained attempt to preserve correspondence, organise records and publish arguments concerning institutional accountability. That work can be examined as evidence of persistence and creative effort. The author’s descriptions of the personal costs involved remain testimony unless independently corroborated. Sources: Barrandodger repository; The Witness Remains Gospel. A/C

The important lesson is not that suffering automatically proves courage or that opposition automatically proves a person is right. The lesson is that a person’s decision to speak, create or preserve a record should be evaluated on its merits rather than dismissed merely because the person lacks status.

A person may exercise courage by making a complaint through proper channels, by defending someone who is being humiliated, by acknowledging an unpopular truth or by asking a powerful institution to explain a decision. They may also exercise courage by deciding not to escalate a conflict when restraint is the responsible course.

Courage is not measured by how many enemies a person believes they have. It is measured by whether they act responsibly when doing so is difficult.

Chapter 18. Intellectual independence: the freedom to examine

Intellectual independence means the ability to examine information rather than accepting it solely because it comes from a powerful, popular or familiar source. It requires curiosity, evidence, reasoning and an openness to correction.

Musk’s technological ventures, Trump’s political influence and Dodger’s documentary work represent three different environments in which information is produced and circulated. None should be exempt from independent examination. The ordinary reader should be able to ask what a claim is based on, who produced the information, what evidence supports it and what alternative explanations remain possible.

The archive’s evidence classifications provide one method of doing this. By distinguishing inspected facts, identified records, personal testimony, corroborated testimony, inference, unresolved allegations, spiritual testimony, formal determinations, historical correspondence and unverified predictions, the collection provides a vocabulary for examining different kinds of claims.

The method should be applied consistently. It must not be used to elevate the author’s own conclusions above scrutiny. Its value is precisely that it makes scrutiny possible.

Intellectual independence does not mean believing the opposite of whatever an authority says. That would merely replace one form of dependence with another. It means evaluating each proposition according to the evidence available.

The ordinary person has the capacity to develop this discipline. They can read beyond a headline, compare original sources, question persuasive language and recognise the difference between evidence and speculation. They do not need a university appointment to begin thinking critically.

Chapter 19. Accountability: answering for the consequences of power

Accountability is the obligation to explain conduct and accept appropriate scrutiny. It applies to public officials, corporate executives, professionals, institutions and individuals.

The 2026 GAO report concerning DOGE illustrates why accountability requires accessible records. It identifies limitations in the information supplied by the Executive Office of the President and several agencies, which prevented the GAO from determining the full extent of some ethics and records-management requirements. This is an institutional transparency issue identified by an independent government auditor. It is not proof that every person associated with DOGE acted improperly. Source: GAO-26-108403. A/H

The archive’s own accountability questions concern whether records are preserved, whether decisions can be explained and whether people affected by institutional decisions can obtain meaningful responses. These questions are legitimate subjects for documentary examination even where the author’s broader allegations remain unresolved.

For an ordinary person, accountability begins with recognising that one’s actions affect others. It includes admitting mistakes, correcting harm where possible, respecting boundaries and accepting that other people are entitled to question one’s conduct.

The principle is universal. No one should demand accountability from others while refusing to accept it themselves.

Chapter 20. Creativity, wisdom, resilience and hope

Creativity is the capacity to make something that did not previously exist in that form. It can appear in art, writing, music, research, invention, problem-solving and everyday acts of construction. It does not require a person to own a company or receive institutional permission.

The publication of Recovered, Not Cured in 2003 and the later development of the Barrandodger repository provide two identifiable stages of creative and documentary production. The first demonstrates a published combination of narrative and illustration. The second demonstrates the use of digital infrastructure to organise and publish a larger collection. The two works are different in form, scale and purpose, but both demonstrate that creative agency can continue across changing technologies and circumstances. Sources: 2003 book record; public archive. A

Wisdom is the ability to consider consequences, recognise uncertainty and distinguish what can be done from what should be done. It is not identical to intelligence, education or technical expertise. A person may know how to accomplish something and still need to consider whether the action is responsible. The distinction applies to technological innovation, political leadership, documentary publication and personal relationships.

Resilience is the capacity to continue adapting and pursuing meaningful activity in difficult circumstances. It should not be romanticised as an obligation to endure preventable suffering. People deserve practical assistance, fair treatment and opportunities to improve their circumstances. Resilience is not a substitute for justice or social support.

Hope is the ability to imagine that present circumstances do not determine every future possibility. It does not require a guarantee of success. It is a reason to continue learning, creating and seeking meaningful relationships even when outcomes remain uncertain.

These qualities belong to human beings, not to a particular economic class. Their expression varies according to opportunity, health, circumstance, support and personal choice. But they are not privileges reserved for those who can afford to display them publicly.

BOOK VII — THE MORAL PRISON OF COMPARISON

Chapter 21. When wealth becomes a measure of worth

There is a psychological and social consequence when a society repeatedly presents extraordinary wealth as the most visible evidence of achievement. People can begin to compare their private lives with the public success of individuals whose circumstances, resources and opportunities are fundamentally different. The comparison is structurally unequal. The billionaire’s resources may provide access to education, professional assistance, investment, legal representation, technology and networks that are unavailable to most people. The president’s office provides institutional authority that ordinary citizens do not possess. The ordinary person may have to devote most of their energy to maintaining basic security.

The mistake is to interpret these unequal circumstances as evidence of unequal human value.

An individual who earns less money has not necessarily contributed less to humanity. A person who has never founded a company may still have raised children, cared for relatives, created art, helped a community, protected another person or made a meaningful intellectual contribution. Those activities may be poorly represented in economic statistics, but they remain part of the substance of human life.

The comparison becomes particularly damaging when people begin to believe that they must become famous before their opinions matter. The public visibility of Musk and Trump can make their statements appear disproportionately important, while the experiences of people without large audiences remain less visible. Yet visibility and validity are not the same thing. An ordinary person’s account should be examined according to its evidence, not dismissed because the speaker lacks celebrity.

The archive’s existence provides a practical illustration. It is a publicly accessible collection developed outside the conventional structures of a major publishing house or government institution. Its creator has used existing digital infrastructure to make material available for examination. The collection’s claims must be evaluated individually, but the ability to publish and preserve work is itself an example of participation in public life. Source: Barrandodger public repository.

The ordinary person must therefore resist the idea that their life is a failed version of somebody else’s success. Their circumstances may be difficult. Their opportunities may be restricted. Their contribution may be less visible. None of those conditions establishes that their existence has less meaning.

Chapter 22. The documentary trap of external approval

A further danger arises when people begin to depend upon institutions or influential individuals to validate their entire identity. Institutions have legitimate roles. Courts determine legal questions. Universities assess academic qualifications. Employers assess work. Regulators make decisions within their jurisdictions. Yet none of these institutions has the authority to determine the complete human worth of the people who interact with them.

The archive repeatedly explores the author’s perception that institutional decisions and responses have affected his independence, public identity and ability to obtain recognition. The document The Witness Remains — Gospel of the Body Left in the Room brings together this testimony and the associated documentary collection. It describes what the author calls the Documentary Trap: the situation in which a person attempts to obtain an answer through records and administrative pathways but feels that the process itself becomes an obstacle to resolving the underlying concern. This is the author’s interpretation, not an established finding of a coordinated institutional design. Source: The Witness Remains — Gospel of the Body Left in the Room. C/G

The philosophical issue is nevertheless broader than the author’s particular circumstances. Administrative decisions can have substantial consequences for individuals. An institution may control access to a service, a payment, an application process or a formal review. This creates an imbalance of practical power. The person affected may have limited resources with which to challenge a decision.

A fair system should provide understandable reasons, accessible review procedures and appropriate opportunities to correct errors. An individual should not have to surrender their dignity merely because another party controls an administrative process.

But the reverse is also important: a person should not interpret every refusal, disagreement or adverse decision as proof of malicious intent. The evidence must establish what happened and support any conclusion about why it happened. This is how the Gospel can defend individual dignity without abandoning impartiality.

The moral prison of external approval begins when a person believes that an institution’s opinion is the final authority on whether they deserve respect. It is possible to acknowledge a decision, challenge it where appropriate and continue to recognise one’s own humanity independently of the outcome.

Chapter 23. The difference between confidence and infallibility

The culture of celebrity often rewards certainty. A confident declaration can attract attention, while a careful qualification may appear less impressive. Yet confidence is not a substitute for knowledge. The person who speaks most loudly is not necessarily the person who understands the subject most deeply.

This is relevant to Musk, Trump and Dodger alike. Each has a public identity involving strong ideas, declarations or visions. The appropriate question is not whether confidence is good or bad. It is whether the person remains answerable to evidence and whether their public assertions can withstand examination.

The archive’s prophetic writing sometimes uses absolute and elevated language. Its spiritual declarations express the author’s understanding of purpose, faith and divine justice. They should be preserved as literary and spiritual testimony, but not treated as independent verification of the events to which they refer. The author is entitled to express a religious conviction. The reader is entitled to distinguish that conviction from a factual determination.

The ability to revise a conclusion is not an admission of personal worthlessness. It is a sign that the person recognises reality as larger than their own initial interpretation.

The ordinary person should not be intimidated by confident public figures, but neither should they be encouraged to replace one unquestionable authority with another. The freedom to think independently includes the freedom to question the author of this Gospel.

BOOK VIII — THE ORDINARY PERSON’S AUTHORITY

Chapter 24. The authority of conscience

The ordinary person may have little formal authority, but they are not without moral agency. Conscience is the capacity to consider the moral significance of an action and recognise that some choices affect the dignity and wellbeing of others.

A person does not need to be a judge to recognise cruelty. They do not need to be a minister to understand that public decisions should be accountable. They do not need to be a billionaire to recognise exploitation. They do not need to be a university professor to ask whether a claim is supported by evidence.

Conscience does not guarantee that a person will always reach the correct conclusion. People can misunderstand situations, act on incomplete information or be influenced by fear and prejudice. But the capacity to examine one’s own actions and reconsider one’s assumptions is a meaningful form of personal authority.

This authority operates in everyday situations. A worker can refuse to falsify a record. A student can question a misleading explanation. A neighbour can intervene responsibly when another person needs help. A citizen can request public information. A person in a difficult administrative situation can ask for a written explanation of a decision.

These acts may appear small when compared with the power of a president or a corporation. Yet democratic society depends upon people exercising precisely these capacities.

Chapter 25. The power to create without permission

One of the strongest examples in the archive is the transition from a published book in 2003 to the large public repository developed decades later. The forms are different, but the underlying capacity to produce and communicate ideas is continuous.

The author did not need to own Allen & Unwin to have a book published. He did not need to own GitHub to create a repository. He did not need to own the internet to make documents accessible. The infrastructure was supplied by others, but the intellectual and creative work required to assemble the material was undertaken through his own effort.

This distinction is important because ordinary people frequently underestimate what they can do with resources already available to them. A person may begin with a notebook, a camera, a public library, a community organisation or a free publishing platform. The result may be modest. It may be incomplete. It may require years of development. But creation is not reserved for people who can purchase a company.

The archive’s value should be assessed by its accuracy, accessibility, originality, preservation quality and public usefulness, not simply by its size. Nevertheless, its development provides an identifiable example of an individual using available technology to establish a substantial public body of work.

The lesson is not that everyone should build an archive of thousands of files. It is that every person has the possibility of creating something that expresses their understanding, contributes to a conversation or assists another person.

Chapter 26. The power to say no

Personal agency also includes the capacity to establish boundaries. The ability to refuse an unreasonable demand, decline an unwanted relationship, challenge an arrangement or request a change is important to personal dignity.

The exercise of this capacity must occur within the law and with appropriate regard for the rights of others. It does not mean that every refusal will be accepted or that every preferred outcome can be achieved. Institutions and other people have their own lawful rights and responsibilities. But a person’s wishes should not be treated as meaningless simply because another party possesses greater financial or organisational power.

The difference between choice and control is important. Genuine choice requires understandable information and realistic alternatives. A person cannot meaningfully choose between options that are inaccessible or impossible to exercise. This is a question of practical agency, not simply the formal existence of a choice on paper.

The wider message of this Gospel is that personal boundaries and self-determination are legitimate human interests. A person does not have to be wealthy to express a preference, ask a question or request that their wishes be considered.

Chapter 27. The power to protect another person’s dignity

The moral value of an action is not always determined by its scale. A person who prevents another from being humiliated may change only one moment, but that moment matters.
