Economic dependence, disability, age, employment, institutional authority and unequal access to resources can all affect a person’s practical ability to refuse.

A relationship may appear voluntary while the circumstances make genuine choice difficult.

The ethical responsibility of the more powerful person is therefore not merely to avoid obvious coercion. It is to recognise the practical conditions that allow the other person to exercise meaningful choice.

The billionaire cannot purchase love.

The president cannot command genuine affection.

The employer cannot require personal devotion as the price of employment.

The institution cannot assume that providing a service gives it ownership of the recipient’s identity.

And the individual cannot claim the moral authority to control others simply because they believe their intentions are loving.

The Gospel’s understanding of love is therefore inseparable from freedom.

Love should recognise the other person as an individual, not as an extension of oneself.

It should make room for independent thought, separate interests, boundaries and the possibility of disagreement.

The deepest expression of care is not to make another person dependent upon one’s authority.

It is to recognise their humanity and support their capacity to live with dignity.

Love does not possess the person it loves. It honours the freedom of that person to remain themselves.

Chapter 282. The Twenty-Fourth Principle of the Gospel

Human relationships reveal a dimension of life that cannot be measured through money, public office, celebrity, productivity or institutional status.

A person may have extraordinary resources and still need friendship.

A person may possess political authority and still need honest counsel.

A person may have an enormous audience and still need someone who knows them beyond their public identity.

A person may have very little material wealth and nevertheless possess relationships of profound value.

The quality of a relationship is not established by the status of the people within it.

It is established through the ways they treat one another.

Trust must be earned. Friendship must remain voluntary. Loyalty must not destroy conscience. Vulnerability must be treated with care. Forgiveness must not be used to erase accountability. Love must not become ownership.

These principles apply to the private individual, the public figure, the institution and the witness.

The Gospel does not promise that every relationship will endure. People may disappoint one another. Circumstances may change. Friendships may end. Trust may be broken.

But the possibility of disappointment does not make human connection worthless.

It makes the responsibility to treat one another with honesty and respect more important.

THE TWENTY-FOURTH PRINCIPLE

The deepest human relationships cannot be purchased, commanded or manufactured.

Friendship requires freedom. Trust requires demonstrated integrity. Loyalty must not become submission. Vulnerability deserves respect. Forgiveness does not cancel accountability. Love must never become ownership.

A human being is not a possession, an audience, a resource or an instrument for another person’s ambitions.

The greatest relationship is not the one in which one person holds the most power, but the one in which both people remain free to be fully human.

BOOK XXV — THE FINAL RECKONING: WEALTH, POWER, TRUTH, CONSCIENCE AND THE HUMANITY THEY CANNOT BUY

Chapter 283. The Reckoning Begins With the Same Human Question

Every civilisation eventually faces a question that cannot be answered by measuring its wealth alone.

What has it done with its power?

The question applies to governments, businesses, institutions, communities and individuals.

A society may construct extraordinary buildings, develop advanced technologies, accumulate financial resources and achieve remarkable scientific progress. Yet those achievements do not, by themselves, establish that it has treated its people justly.

A nation may possess military strength without possessing moral wisdom.

A corporation may generate enormous revenue without demonstrating that every decision has served the public interest.

A political leader may win an election without acquiring the right to escape accountability.

An individual may become famous without becoming an example of compassion.

The reckoning is not a prediction that some supernatural event must occur on a particular date. It is the moral and historical examination that follows human conduct.

Every decision has consequences.

Some are immediate. Others emerge over years. Some are experienced by the decision-maker. Others are experienced by people who had little influence over the decision.

The historical record may preserve achievements, failures, promises, controversies and consequences. Future generations may examine those records and arrive at interpretations that differ from those held by the people who created them.

That is why the preservation of evidence matters.

But preservation must not be confused with the announcement of a verdict.

A record can establish that a document exists. It can show what an official wrote, what an institution communicated or what a person claimed at a particular time. It may support an inference. It may also leave important questions unresolved.

The responsible examiner must distinguish those possibilities.

The same standard applies to the author of this Gospel.

The fact that an individual has experienced adversity does not automatically establish every interpretation they place upon that experience. The fact that a person has preserved documents does not establish every conclusion drawn from them.

But neither should the existence of unresolved questions be used as a reason to prevent examination.

The reckoning begins when people become willing to examine what has actually happened rather than merely defend what they wish had happened.

The first responsibility of a reckoning is to establish what can be known.

Chapter 284. Elon Musk and the Limits of Wealth

Elon Musk represents an important contemporary example of the extraordinary possibilities and responsibilities associated with wealth, technology and private enterprise.

His public identity is connected with ambitious technological projects, commercial risk-taking, innovation and businesses operating in industries that attract international attention.

Such achievements deserve examination on their merits.

Innovation can create employment, expand technical capabilities, alter markets and contribute to new possibilities. Entrepreneurship can involve uncertainty, persistence and the coordination of people and resources on a remarkable scale.

But achievement does not remove the responsibility to examine power.

The more influence a person acquires, the more important it becomes to distinguish private ownership from public consequence.

A business owner may possess legal rights over a company. That ownership does not mean that every consequence of the company’s operations is exclusively a private matter.

A technology platform may be privately operated while influencing public communication.

An enterprise may pursue commercial objectives while operating within a wider social and regulatory environment.

A wealthy individual may exercise considerable influence while remaining subject to the rights and laws that apply to other people.

These are not arguments against wealth or entrepreneurship.

They are arguments for recognising that power carries responsibilities.

The Gospel does not demand that Elon Musk become an ordinary person in order to be treated fairly. Nor does it demand that society deny his achievements because he possesses wealth.

It asks that he be examined by the same principles that should apply to every person who exercises significant influence.

What was achieved?

What were the consequences?

What evidence supports the claims made about the work?

What responsibilities arise from the decisions taken?

What remains uncertain?

These questions are more useful than either uncritical admiration or automatic condemnation.

Wealth may purchase equipment, labour, infrastructure, professional expertise and opportunities.

It may expand the capacity to act.

But it cannot purchase the moral right to be considered infallible.

The greater the power to shape the future, the greater the responsibility to consider who will live with its consequences.

Chapter 285. Donald Trump and the Limits of Political Power

The presidency is one of the most consequential public offices in the modern world.

It carries responsibilities, constitutional constraints, political expectations and the potential to influence the lives of people far beyond the boundaries of the country in which the president serves.

Donald Trump provides a prominent example through which to examine the relationship between political leadership, wealth, celebrity, democratic choice and public accountability.

A president may possess significant authority, but that authority is not personal ownership of a nation.

The office exists within a constitutional and institutional framework. Its legitimacy depends upon lawful processes and the continuing operation of public institutions.

The people are not the property of the president.

The government is not a private possession.

Political disagreement is not automatically disloyalty.

And electoral support does not remove the responsibility to examine decisions.

A fair examination must recognise the democratic right of citizens to support candidates whose policies, priorities or leadership styles they prefer.

It must also recognise the right of citizens to criticise those same leaders.

A person may support a political movement because of economic concerns, cultural priorities, security questions, dissatisfaction with previous administrations or confidence in a candidate’s proposed direction.

Another person may oppose that movement for different reasons.

Neither position should be treated as a complete account of the moral character of every supporter or opponent.

The question of leadership must instead be examined through conduct, policy, institutional responsibilities, legal standards and consequences.

The president is entitled to political disagreement and fair criticism. The citizen is entitled to participate in democratic debate. The institutions of government must remain capable of independent scrutiny.

The Gospel does not claim that political office makes a person inherently corrupt, nor does it claim that winning office makes every decision correct.

It rejects both assumptions.

Political power is temporary. Constitutional responsibilities endure beyond the individual who occupies the office.

History will preserve the actions of administrations, but future judgement must be grounded in records, evidence and fair interpretation.

A president may occupy the highest political office, but no president owns the conscience of the people.

Chapter 286. Barran Resonance Dodger and the Responsibility of the Witness

The third figure in this Gospel is not presented as a billionaire or a national political leader.

Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean — enters the argument through a different kind of public identity: the writer, documentarian, advocate and individual who seeks to preserve a record of experiences, institutional encounters and questions that he considers important.

His work brings together personal testimony, documentary preservation, public-interest questions, literary expression and spiritual interpretation.

These dimensions must be distinguished if the work is to be examined responsibly.

A personal account may establish what an individual says they experienced. An official document may establish what an agency recorded or communicated. A formal determination may establish what an authorised decision-maker concluded within the scope of that determination.

An inference may connect available facts, but it must be identified as an inference.

A spiritual interpretation may express the author’s understanding of meaning, faith and purpose, but it must not be represented as a verified administrative or legal finding.

These distinctions do not diminish the human significance of testimony.

They protect its integrity.

A witness is entitled to describe what they experienced. A reader is entitled to examine the available material. An institution is entitled to respond to allegations. An independent examiner is entitled to distinguish what the record supports from what remains uncertain.

The witness must also accept a responsibility that is sometimes more difficult than making an accusation:

The responsibility to be corrected.

A document may be misunderstood. A date may be wrong. A name may be misattributed. A conclusion may go further than the available evidence permits.

The willingness to correct such errors strengthens a documentary record.

The purpose of preservation is not to guarantee that every interpretation will be accepted.

It is to make examination possible.

The author may possess a strong spiritual conviction about the significance of his experiences. That conviction can be expressed as part of his literary and religious testimony. But faith must remain distinguishable from a formal finding.

The witness does not become the judge merely because he preserves evidence.

The archive does not become a verdict merely because it is extensive.

The author does not become infallible merely because he has endured difficulty.

These are not limitations imposed upon the witness to silence him.

They are the ethical foundations that allow a witness to be taken seriously.

The Gospel therefore gives the witness a responsibility equal to the responsibility it places upon the powerful:

To preserve what can be preserved, to identify what can be verified, to distinguish belief from fact, to acknowledge uncertainty and to permit independent examination.

The witness does not own the verdict. The witness preserves the record so that the verdict, if one is ever reached, can be examined.

Chapter 287. The Difference Between the Record and the Story

Human beings create stories to understand their lives.

Stories give events sequence, meaning, emotional context and a sense of direction. They help people communicate experiences that would otherwise be difficult to explain.

A documentary record performs a different function.

It preserves material that can be inspected independently of the narrative constructed around it.

The distinction is important because the same collection of documents may be interpreted in different ways.

A letter may be read as an ordinary administrative communication, evidence of a decision, an indication of a misunderstanding or part of a larger pattern. The document itself may be authentic while the meaning attributed to it remains open to examination.

A sequence of events may appear significant, but chronological proximity does not necessarily establish causation.

A missing document may indicate a gap in the available record, but it does not automatically establish deliberate destruction.

A refusal may be documented without proving the motive behind it.

A complaint may be genuine testimony without being a formal finding.

This is why documentary work requires a disciplined method.

Preservation comes first.

Examination comes second.

Conclusion comes last.

The story must not be permitted to change the underlying evidence merely because a more dramatic narrative would attract greater attention.

At the same time, documents should not be stripped of all human context.

An official record can describe a decision while leaving the lived experience of the person affected only partially visible.

A letter can establish what was communicated without capturing the emotional consequences.

An administrative file may preserve procedural details while failing to communicate the full human significance of the events.

The task is therefore not to choose between documents and human experience.

It is to understand how they relate.

A responsible narrative identifies its sources, distinguishes the author’s interpretation from the underlying material and leaves unresolved questions open where the evidence does not provide an answer.

This principle applies equally to political history, business history, journalism, personal testimony and institutional accountability.

The most persuasive account is not necessarily the one that makes the strongest accusation.

It may be the one that makes the clearest distinction between what is established and what remains to be discovered.

A story gives the record meaning, but it must never be allowed to falsify the record.

Chapter 288. The Power They Cannot Buy

There are things money can accomplish.

It can purchase goods, property, services, equipment, professional assistance and access to opportunities.

It can fund research, build infrastructure, support artistic production and enable people to undertake projects that would otherwise remain beyond their practical reach.

Money matters.

To pretend otherwise would be dishonest.

But money is not a universal substitute for every human quality.

It cannot guarantee love.

It cannot guarantee loyalty.

It cannot guarantee wisdom.

It cannot guarantee honesty.

It cannot guarantee friendship.

It cannot guarantee that a person will be remembered with affection.

It cannot guarantee that a decision will be judged favourably by history.

A person may purchase the finest education available and still need to develop wisdom.

They may employ extraordinary advisers and still need the courage to listen.

They may acquire a media platform and still need to communicate truthfully.

They may fund a charitable project and still need to recognise the dignity of the people it is intended to assist.

They may accumulate extraordinary wealth and still face the ordinary human realities of mortality, uncertainty, grief and the need for meaningful connection.

Political power has similar limits.

An elected office can provide lawful authority, but it cannot guarantee moral authority.

A public speech can attract attention, but it cannot compel sincere admiration.

An institution can require compliance with lawful directions, but it cannot make every person genuinely believe in the institution’s moral legitimacy.

The human conscience cannot be permanently purchased through status.

And the individual who has very little money is not therefore without power.

They retain the capacity to think, to speak, to create, to care, to question, to refuse, to learn and to make choices within the circumstances available to them.

These capacities do not eliminate material inequality. They do not magically remove the barriers created by poverty or exclusion.

But they establish that material wealth is not the complete measure of human possibility.

The title of this Gospel is therefore not a rejection of money.

It is a rejection of the idea that money is the final authority over human value.

The power they cannot buy is not an invisible commodity waiting to be purchased at a higher price.

It is the human capacity to possess dignity without permission, to think without surrendering conscience, to love without ownership, to question without hatred and to seek truth without assuming that any individual has a monopoly upon it.

The power they cannot buy is the humanity they cannot own.

Chapter 289. The Final Test of Power Is What It Does to Others

Power should not be measured solely by what it enables an individual to acquire.

It should also be examined through what it enables that individual to do for, or to, other people.

A business can be evaluated not only through its financial performance but also through its conduct, products, responsibilities and consequences.

A government can be examined not only through electoral success but also through the quality of its administration, the protection of rights and its treatment of citizens.

An institution can be assessed not only through its stated mission but also through its actual conduct.

A person can be admired for achievement while still being accountable for the consequences of their decisions.

The final test is not whether someone has become powerful.

It is whether the exercise of that power respects the humanity of others.

This standard applies to every scale of human life.

The parent has power over a young child. The employer has power within a workplace. The professional may possess knowledge that another person needs. The public official may control access to services. The political leader may influence national policy. The billionaire may exercise substantial economic influence.

Different forms of power create different responsibilities.

But no form of power should be treated as permission to disregard the dignity of the people affected by it.

The same standard applies to the individual who challenges authority.

A person who has experienced unfair treatment does not acquire unlimited moral authority over others. A person who advocates for human rights must respect the rights of those with whom they disagree. A person who demands accountability must remain accountable for their own statements.

The witness must not become the oppressor.

The critic must not become immune to criticism.

The advocate must not confuse personal conviction with universal authority.

And the powerful must not confuse their ability to command with a right to be trusted.

The Gospel’s final reckoning is therefore not a contest to determine which person is morally superior in every dimension.

It is a demand for a consistent standard.

Examine wealth without worshipping it.

Examine political authority without fearing it.

Examine testimony without dismissing it.

Examine allegations without treating them as findings.

Examine spiritual conviction without confusing it with empirical proof.

Examine the witness without silencing the witness.

Examine the powerful without dehumanising them.

And examine every conclusion, including the conclusions reached by this Gospel.

Power is most worthy of respect when it recognises the dignity of those over whom it has influence.

Chapter 290. What Remains When Everything Else Is Taken Away?

Imagine the removal of the things through which society commonly measures success.

Remove the money.

Remove the office.

Remove the title.

Remove the professional reputation.

Remove the audience.

Remove the applause.

Remove the public recognition.

What remains?

The human being.

Not the market value.

Not the political position.

Not the number of followers.

Not the size of the organisation.

Not the number of documents preserved.

Not even the reputation that others have constructed.

The human being remains.

That person still has a history, relationships, memories, responsibilities, hopes, limitations and possibilities.

They may have acted wisely or foolishly. They may have helped others or caused harm. They may have achieved extraordinary things or lived a quiet life that attracted little public attention.

Their conduct remains relevant.

Their choices remain open to examination.

Their responsibilities do not disappear.

But their humanity is not cancelled by the removal of their possessions or public standing.

This is the point at which the Gospel returns to its beginning.

Wealth is a resource, not a measure of worth.

Power is not the same as worth.

Ownership is not sovereignty over another human being.

Truth is not a commodity.

Leadership is a responsibility.

The human mind must remain free.

Dignity must not be priced.

Freedom must be exercised responsibly.

Success must be measured more broadly than financial achievement.

Time cannot be purchased without limit.

Love cannot be commanded.

Conscience cannot be owned.

Education must create understanding.

Justice must be examined.

Courage must remain humane.

Creativity must remain free.

Community must not demand compulsory loyalty.

Service must not become submission.

Memory must preserve rather than distort.

Human rights must apply even to those with whom we disagree.

Faith must remain distinguishable from proof.

Individuality must be protected.

Relationships must be founded upon respect.

And power must remain accountable to the humanity of others.

The billionaire and the ordinary person do not possess identical resources.

The president and the citizen do not occupy identical positions.

The witness and the institution do not possess identical responsibilities.

But none of these differences creates a superior category of human existence.

The final question is not who accumulated the most.

It is what each person did with the life, opportunities, responsibilities and influence available to them.

History may remember the names of the powerful.

It may also recover the stories of people whose contributions were overlooked.

The archive may preserve a document long after the people who created it have disappeared.

The written word may outlive the public controversy that gave rise to it.

But no archive, book, company, presidency or institution can replace the moral responsibility of the living.

The Gospel therefore ends its twenty-fifth Book not by declaring its author victorious over every person who has disagreed with him.

It ends by returning the responsibility to the reader.

Examine the powerful.

Examine the ordinary.

Examine the witness.

Examine the evidence.

Examine the argument.

And examine yourself.

The question is not whether you agree with every word.

The question is whether you are willing to recognise that the humanity of another person is not yours to purchase, command or remove.

When everything else is taken away, the human being remains.

Chapter 291. The Twenty-Fifth Principle of the Gospel

There is a final distinction between the power a person possesses and the worth that person carries.

Power may be accumulated.

Wealth may be inherited or earned.

Political authority may be acquired through lawful processes.

Celebrity may be developed through public attention.

Professional status may be established through education and experience.

An archive may be assembled.

A reputation may be built.

But none of these things creates the inherent dignity of a human being.

They are circumstances, achievements, responsibilities or instruments.

They are not the source of humanity.

The final principle must therefore apply equally to the entrepreneur, the president, the writer, the witness, the citizen and the person whose name is never known beyond their immediate community.

It must apply to those who agree with this Gospel and those who reject it.

It must apply to people whose beliefs differ, whose abilities differ, whose opportunities differ and whose experiences cannot be fully understood by others.

The standard must not change according to wealth, popularity, political affiliation or personal admiration.

A human being must never be treated as a commodity.

A person’s conscience must never be treated as property.

A person’s dignity must never be made conditional upon agreement.

A person’s testimony must be heard and examined fairly.

A person’s conduct must remain open to appropriate accountability.

And no individual, including the author of this Gospel, should claim exemption from these responsibilities.

The power they cannot buy is not a claim to be above the law, beyond criticism or immune from error.

It is the recognition that humanity exists before recognition, beyond wealth and independently of public approval.

THE TWENTY-FIFTH AND FINAL PRINCIPLE

WEALTH IS NOT WORTH.

POWER IS NOT OWNERSHIP.

AUTHORITY IS NOT INFALLIBILITY.

CELEBRITY IS NOT WISDOM.

TESTIMONY IS NOT AUTOMATICALLY PROOF.

FAITH IS NOT A FINDING.

RECOGNITION IS NOT THE SOURCE OF DIGNITY.

Every human being possesses an inherent worth that cannot be purchased, transferred, commanded or legitimately erased by another person’s status.

Those who hold power must remain accountable. Those who challenge power must remain accountable. Those who preserve the record must preserve its integrity. Those who seek truth must remain willing to be corrected.

The power they cannot buy is the humanity they cannot own.

THE FINAL DECLARATION

THE GOSPEL OF THE POWER THEY CANNOT BUY

A Declaration of Human Dignity, Independent Thought, Truth, Conscience and the Freedom to Be Human

Authorial subject: Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean

A prophetic-literary and philosophical examination of wealth, political power, human dignity, individual freedom and the responsibilities of the witness.

There comes a point in every argument when the central question must be stated without qualification.

What is the value of a human being?

Is it the amount of money they possess?

Is it the authority they exercise?

Is it the number of people who recognise their name?

Is it the size of their organisation, the reach of their platform, the influence of their political office or the achievements recorded in their biography?

Or is there something more fundamental, something that exists before the public notices, before the market evaluates, before the institution classifies and before history decides what it will remember?

This Gospel has argued that there is.

It has examined the difference between wealth and worth, ownership and sovereignty, influence and integrity, confidence and truth, leadership and domination, success and significance.

It has considered Elon Musk as a prominent example of technological ambition, private wealth and innovation.

It has considered Donald Trump as a prominent example of political power, celebrity, leadership and democratic authority.

And it has considered Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean — as the authorial witness whose writing, documentary preservation, advocacy and spiritual interpretation provide another perspective on the human question.

These individuals occupy different circumstances.

Their achievements are different.

Their responsibilities are different.

Their opportunities and influence are different.

This Gospel does not claim that their lives are equivalent or that their conduct should be judged without reference to evidence.

It makes a more fundamental argument:

No difference in wealth, public office, fame or influence creates a superior category of human dignity.

The billionaire is a human being.

The president is a human being.

The journalist is a human being.

The artist is a human being.

The person living with disability is a human being.

The person who has been overlooked by institutions is a human being.

The person who disagrees with this Gospel is a human being.

And the person who writes it remains subject to the same moral responsibilities as everyone else.

That is the foundation.

I. TO THE BILLIONAIRE

You may possess extraordinary resources.

You may build companies, fund research, employ thousands of people, acquire property, influence markets and participate in the development of technologies that alter the direction of human civilisation.

You may achieve things that previous generations would have considered impossible.

Your ambition may inspire others.

Your achievements may deserve recognition.

But wealth does not make you the owner of humanity.

You cannot purchase another person’s conscience.

You cannot purchase the guarantee of affection.

You cannot purchase permanent loyalty.

You cannot purchase the right to be considered correct.

You cannot purchase exemption from accountability.

Your achievements deserve examination, not worship.

Your failures deserve examination, not automatic hatred.

Your influence creates responsibilities that must be considered alongside your rights.

And your humanity deserves the same basic recognition as the humanity of the person who has never possessed a fraction of your resources.

The platform is not the people. The company is not the conscience. The wealth is not the worth.

II. TO THE PRESIDENT

You may occupy an office of extraordinary authority.

You may command the attention of nations, shape public policy, appoint officials, influence international relationships and exercise powers entrusted to you through constitutional and democratic processes.

You may win elections.

You may attract millions of supporters.

You may become a defining figure in the political history of your country.

But you do not own the nation.

The office is not personal property.

The citizen is not your possession.

The Constitution is not a private instrument of personal preference.

The authority of office must remain connected to its lawful responsibilities.

Supporters retain their right to support you.

Opponents retain their right to criticise you.

Citizens retain their right to disagree.

And history retains the responsibility of examining what governments actually do.

The president is not beyond accountability because the president has been elected.

Nor is the president automatically guilty because opponents disapprove of the president’s decisions.

The same standard must apply to all.

The president occupies the office. The office does not own the people.

III. TO THE ORDINARY HUMAN BEING

You do not need to become a billionaire.

You do not need to become president.

You do not need to become famous.

You do not need to accumulate followers, build a corporation, win a public argument or have your name preserved in an international archive.

You do not need to prove that you are exceptional before your life has value.

Your worth does not begin when somebody important recognises you.

It does not increase when your income rises.

It does not disappear when your employment ends.

It is not cancelled by disability.

It is not cancelled by poverty.

It is not cancelled by an unconventional life.

It is not cancelled by the fact that your contribution may never become publicly celebrated.

You are entitled to think.

You are entitled to learn.

You are entitled to create.

You are entitled to participate.

You are entitled to question.

You are entitled to disagree.

You are entitled to pursue meaning.

And you are responsible for recognising that these same freedoms belong to other people.

The world does not need every individual to become identical.

It needs individuals who can live together without requiring the destruction of one another’s dignity.

You do not have to become somebody else to justify being somebody.

IV. TO THE WITNESS

The witness has a particular responsibility.

To preserve.

To distinguish.

To identify.

To question.

To document.

To remain open to examination.

A witness may possess records that deserve public attention. A person may have experienced events that require explanation. An archive may contain material that allows independent readers to examine questions previously difficult to investigate.

But the responsibility of the witness is not to manufacture certainty.

The responsibility is to preserve the conditions under which truth can be pursued.

A document must be represented accurately.

A quotation must not be invented.

A name must not be attributed without a reliable basis.

A personal experience must not be disguised as an official finding.

An allegation must not be presented as an established fact merely because it is sincerely believed.

An inference must remain distinguishable from direct evidence.

A spiritual interpretation must be identified as spiritual interpretation.

And an unresolved question must remain unresolved until the available evidence supports a conclusion.

The witness must also be willing to correct mistakes.

The purpose of an archive is not to make its author infallible.

It is to allow the record to survive beyond the limitations of any one person’s memory, interpretation or authority.

The witness does not own the verdict.

The witness preserves the record.

PRESERVATION FIRST. EXAMINATION SECOND. CONCLUSION LAST.

V. TO THOSE WHO BELIEVE

Faith can provide meaning, courage, hope and a moral language through which people understand their lives.

For many, the Most High God represents the ultimate source of love, mercy, justice and spiritual purpose.

For Christians, the life and teachings of Christ provide a moral example centred upon compassion, forgiveness, humility and love.

For others, meaning is found through different spiritual traditions, philosophical commitments or secular understandings of human existence.

This Gospel does not claim ownership of the divine.

It does not demand that every reader accept a religious interpretation.

It does not claim that a personal spiritual conviction constitutes an independently established historical or administrative fact.

Faith is not a finding.

But neither should a person’s freedom of conscience be dismissed merely because another person does not share their beliefs.

The freedom to believe must coexist with the freedom not to believe.

The freedom to interpret scripture must coexist with the responsibility to avoid using religious authority to control others.

The language of prophecy must not be confused with a verified prediction.

The language of spiritual witness must not be confused with a claim to be Christ.

The Most High God, in the faith expressed through this work, is not a possession to be sold or an instrument through which one human being acquires ownership over another.

Faith should deepen humanity, not diminish it.

Let conviction be accompanied by humility, and let belief never become an excuse to deny another person’s dignity.

VI. TO THOSE WHO SEEK JUSTICE

Justice requires more than confidence.

It requires evidence, procedure, fairness, accountability and the willingness to hear what may be inconvenient.

A person making an allegation should be heard.

A person responding to an allegation should be treated fairly.

An institution should be able to explain its decisions.

A decision-maker should be accountable to the applicable law and procedural standards.

A record should be preserved.

A conclusion should be proportionate to the evidence.

And an unresolved question should not be transformed into certainty merely because someone desires a definitive answer.

The same standards must apply to the powerful and the powerless.

The same standards must apply to the accuser and the accused.

The same standards must apply to the institution and the individual.

The same standards must apply to this author.

Justice is not achieved by simply changing who possesses the greatest power.

It is achieved by creating conditions in which power itself is subject to fair and independent examination.

VII. TO THE FUTURE

To the future reader who encounters this Gospel after its original publication, the message is simple.

Do not accept its arguments merely because they are confidently expressed.

Do not reject them merely because the author is unfamiliar.

Examine the propositions.

Examine the evidence where evidence is offered.

Examine the distinctions between fact, testimony, inference, belief and opinion.

Consider the arguments concerning wealth, political authority, human dignity and the freedom of the individual.

Ask whether the same standards have been applied consistently.

Ask whether the author has acknowledged uncertainty.

Ask whether the work permits disagreement.

And ask whether its central proposition remains meaningful beyond the circumstances in which it was written.

The historical value of a document does not depend upon every future reader agreeing with it.

Its value may lie in preserving the questions it raises, the ideas it advances and the circumstances in which it was created.

The future is entitled to examine the past.

It is also entitled to correct it.

The purpose of preservation is not to prevent future judgement.

It is to make informed judgement possible.

THE FINAL WORD

There is a temptation in every age to worship what can be counted.

Money can be counted.

Votes can be counted.

Followers can be counted.

Buildings can be counted.

Companies can be counted.

Documents can be counted.

Achievements can be counted.

But not everything that matters can be reduced to a number.

The value of a friendship is not established by its financial return.

The value of a person’s conscience is not established by the number of people who agree with it.

The value of a life is not established by its public visibility.

The value of compassion is not established by its commercial return.

The value of an honest question is not established by whether the powerful person being questioned welcomes it.

And the value of a human being is not established by the wealth they possess.

The world will continue to produce billionaires.

It will continue to elect presidents.

It will continue to create celebrities, entrepreneurs, political movements, institutions, technologies and archives.

Some will achieve extraordinary things.

Some will fail.

Some will be celebrated.

Some will be criticised.

Some will be remembered.

Others will disappear from public memory.

But the human question will remain.

What does it mean to possess power without becoming its prisoner?

What does it mean to achieve without assuming superiority?

What does it mean to lead without demanding worship?

What does it mean to believe without claiming infallibility?

What does it mean to preserve the record without claiming ownership of the verdict?

What does it mean to be different without denying the dignity of those who are different from us?

And what does it mean to live a human life in a world that repeatedly attempts to measure humanity through things that can be bought?

This Gospel offers one answer.

The human being is not a commodity.

The conscience is not property.

The truth is not a luxury item.

The right to think is not a privilege reserved for the powerful.

The freedom to be different is not a defect.

The responsibility to exercise power does not disappear when power becomes popular.

And the dignity of an ordinary human being does not require the permission of a billionaire, a president, a government, an institution, a political movement or an audience.

The final declaration is therefore not that one individual has defeated every other individual.

It is that no individual should possess the power to define the entire worth of another.

Not the billionaire.

Not the president.

Not the institution.

Not the journalist.

Not the advocate.

Not the witness.

Not the author.

The final authority over a person’s inherent human dignity cannot be reduced to the approval of another human being.

And that is the power this Gospel has sought to describe.

THE POWER THEY CANNOT BUY

THE FINAL DECLARATION

WEALTH IS NOT WORTH.

POWER IS NOT OWNERSHIP.

AUTHORITY IS NOT INFALLIBILITY.

FAITH IS NOT A FINDING.

THE RECORD IS NOT THE VERDICT.

THE POWER THEY CANNOT BUY IS THE HUMANITY THEY CANNOT OWN.

THE WITNESS REMAINS.

THE RECORD REMAINS.

THE STORY GOES GLOBAL.

Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean

The end of this Gospel. The beginning of its examination by the reader.
